# DOCUMENT 03 — GP Podiatry — 2017-07-06

CLIN-000899  # 6 July 2017 — GP Podiatry referral / prioritisation note

CLIN-000900  **Source type:** primary contemporaneous GP clinical record  
CLIN-000901  **Practice:** Galway East Medical Practice  
CLIN-000902  **Date:** 6 July 2017  
CLIN-000903  **Provenance:** extracted from the 2017 GP record PDF supplied directly to the project on 12 August 2026.  
CLIN-000904  **Status:** faithful transcription of the relevant entry. The original PDF remains controlling. No analytical findings are made on this page.

---

CLIN-000905  > **06/07/2017 A66: Referral to other provider/nurse/therapist/social worker - Phoned podiatry to discuss case - will mark as priority case and as Parkinson's - should be called mid September**

---

CLIN-000906  ## Source-status note

CLIN-000907  This entry directly establishes that on 6 July 2017 the GP contacted Podiatry about Liam's case and recorded that the case would be marked as a priority **“as Parkinson's.”**

CLIN-000908  It does not by itself establish:

CLIN-000909  - the exact wording of any written referral;
CLIN-000910  - what clinical detail Podiatry received beyond the telephone discussion;
CLIN-000911  - whether Podiatry understood its role as adjunctive structural care, principal explanation of the foot problem, or something else;
CLIN-000912  - what treatment was later provided or what information returned to Neurology or the GP.

CLIN-001715  **Recipient:** Prof Dan Healy, Consultant Neurologist, Beaumont Hospital  
CLIN-001716  **Patient copied:** yes, as shown on the reproduced page  
CLIN-001717  **Date:** 2017 context; the exact date is **not visible on the reproduced page currently held in the project**  
CLIN-001718  **Provenance:** image attachment (`image.png`) located in the connected Gmail account and directly visually inspected. The image reproduces a Galway University Hospitals clinician letter/page concerning Liam Moloney.  
CLIN-001719  **Status:** faithful redacted transcription of the visible substantive correspondence. The source image remains controlling. No analytical findings are made on this page.

CLIN-001720  **CC: Prof Dan Healy, Consultant Neurologist, Beaumont Hospital, Dublin 9**

CLIN-001721  Dear Dan,

CLIN-001722  I wonder if I might seek your advice please as regards Mr Moloney.

CLIN-001723  Liam is remarkable young man who progressed very far on Channel 4s Countdown in recent years.

CLIN-001724  Unfortunately Liam seems to have had symptoms of Parkinson's for the last 13 years. He is now age 39 at current presentation, and this is his first time being seen by Neurology.

CLIN-001725  I would be grateful for advice on what genetic testing might be appropriate in the circumstances and more particularly, whether you thing this is reasonable and likely to help Liam?

CLIN-001726  In particular would you have genetic contacts who might analyse DNA? UHG will pay wherever the NCMG send samples of course. I have looked at Centogenes proposals as enclosed.

CLIN-001727  I have his imaging enclosed on disc.

CLIN-001728  If you felt it might be of benefit to him to be seen in Dublin, Liam would be happy to be seen publically or privately for a once off opinion.

CLIN-001729  **Myself and Tim are keen to hold back on Levodopa insofar as maybe possible unless you'd advise otherwise?**

CLIN-001730  We have made a Video which Liam had consented to for Clinical Purposes only - - - and this includes the condition of provision to Beaumont for a second opinion.

CLIN-001731  With thanks and kind regards,

CLIN-001732  Thomas

---

CLIN-001733  **Source-status note:** This reproduced contemporaneous page directly supports a consultative/collective element in the levodopa-deferral position and a request to Prof Dan Healy for genetic advice and possible second opinion. The page itself does **not identify “Tim.”** Identification of Prof Timothy Counihan as having given advice in 2017 comes from the separate 29 July 2025 Monaghan submission to the Medical Council. The exact original date/header and any surrounding pages should still be obtained if available.

# DOCUMENT 06 — Healy Response — 2017-09-12

CLIN-001734  # 12 September 2017 — Prof Daniel G. Healy response after specialist review

CLIN-001735  **Source type:** primary contemporaneous specialist correspondence  
CLIN-001736  **Author:** Prof Daniel G. Healy, Consultant Neurologist  
CLIN-001737  **Recipient:** Dr Thomas Monaghan, Consultant Neurologist, Galway University Hospital

CLIN-001741  Dear Tom,

CLIN-001742  > “Thank you for asking me to see this man who I think probably has Park II and I would suggest testing that initially. If it is negative I would go on to test a panel.”

CLIN-001743  > “It is a little bit unclear but he probably developed Parkinsonism somewhere between his late 20s and early 30s.”

CLIN-001744  The letter then discusses background/family-history considerations and possible heterozygous manifestations. Some words in that paragraph are not reproduced here because the supplied image is not sufficiently clear to guarantee a literal transcription of every word.

CLIN-001745  > “There are very few non motor symptoms. He was able to detect 7/10 smells on the UPSIT scale. He describes a dramatic improvement in his motor symptoms with Procyclidine low dose qds and Rasagiline 1mg od. He takes Clonazepam at night.”

CLIN-001746  Prof Healy records review of the MRI brain and advises keeping an open mind about a subtle imaging point if PARK II testing is negative. The exact wording of that imaging sentence should be taken from the original image rather than this partial transcript.

CLIN-001747  > “He had a lot of questions which I answered to the best of my ability.”

CLIN-001748  > “Physical activity would be helpful and as you are aware patients with PARK II tend to manage quite well for long periods of time with relatively low doses of Dopamine replacement therapy. They can become quite dyskinetic if over-used. One should mentally think of it as a nigropathy.”

CLIN-001749  > “I have not arranged to see him again. I have given him his CDs back. I would be very interested in the results of the tests and happy to see in the future as required.”

CLIN-001750  Yours sincerely,

CLIN-001751  Prof. Daniel G. Healy  
CLIN-001752  Consultant Neurologist

---

CLIN-001753  ## Source-status note

CLIN-001754  This letter directly establishes what Prof Healy himself recorded after seeing Liam in September 2017. In particular it directly records:

CLIN-001755  - Healy's opinion that Liam **“probably has Park II”**;
CLIN-001756  - his recommendation to test that initially and move to a panel if negative;
CLIN-001757  - his estimate that Parkinsonism probably developed between the late 20s and early 30s;
CLIN-001758  - the reported dramatic motor improvement with Procyclidine and Rasagiline;
CLIN-001759  - his statement that PARK II patients can manage for long periods on relatively low doses of dopamine replacement therapy while becoming dyskinetic if over-used;
CLIN-001760  - his interest in the test results and willingness to review again if required.

CLIN-001761  This source does **not** state that Prof Healy ordered immediate commencement of levodopa. It also does not by itself establish what Galway subsequently did with the advice. Those are separate analytical questions.

# DOCUMENT 07 — Contemporaneous Treatment Response Emails — 2017

CLIN-001762  # Contemporaneous treatment-response emails — October 2017

CLIN-001763  ## Purpose

CLIN-001764  Preserve contemporaneous patient-origin evidence about the effect of the neurological treatment regime before formal propositions are frozen.

CLIN-001765  This file distinguishes:

CLIN-001766  - what Liam wrote contemporaneously in October 2017;
CLIN-001767  - what Liam now recalls about the same episode;
CLIN-001768  - what can safely be inferred;
CLIN-001769  - what still requires the clinical record or expert interpretation.

---

CLIN-001770  ## 1. 4 October 2017 email — direct contemporaneous account during Kemadrin/procyclidine withdrawal

CLIN-001771  **Source:** Gmail, sent by Liam Moloney to Celia Moloney, subject `Here`, 4 October 2017.

CLIN-001772  The email records Monaghan's staged Kemadrin/procyclidine titration and a planned treatment break to assess its effects. Liam wrote that within days of beginning half a tablet there had been significant improvement and described the period as `living in a miracle, literally`.

CLIN-001773  He listed concrete functional changes:

CLIN-001774  - `My walk is back together.`
CLIN-001775  - `My writing is good.`
CLIN-001776  - `I can stand up straight.`
CLIN-001777  - `My legs are loose and limber.`
CLIN-001778  - he could stand while the bus was moving;
CLIN-001779  - lift his bicycle without falling over it;
CLIN-001780  - jump, run, hop and skip;
CLIN-001781  - and perform ordinary dexterity tasks that had previously been difficult.

CLIN-001782  Four days into the Kemadrin break, he wrote:

CLIN-001783  > `I am feeling the tension building up, my feet are restless and my walk is falling apart....but my eyesight is back to normal.`

CLIN-001784  The same email also contains Liam's then-current historical reconstruction of the longstanding foot problem, including recurrent stinging on the outside of the left foot and years of severe burning foot pain. Importantly, it distinguishes foot pain from leg tension in his own experience while also trying to understand their place within the wider Parkinsonian illness.

CLIN-001785  ### Source-status

CLIN-001786  This is contemporaneous patient evidence, written while the withdrawal experiment was actually occurring. It directly supports that:

CLIN-001787  - Kemadrin/procyclidine had produced marked global physical improvement;
CLIN-001788  - a planned withdrawal was being used to assess the medication's effects;
CLIN-001789  - during withdrawal, tension increased, the feet became restless and gait deteriorated;
CLIN-001790  - visual side effects were simultaneously improving off the medication.

CLIN-001791  It does **not**, by itself, prove the mechanism of the foot symptoms or that levodopa would have produced the same effect.

---

CLIN-001792  ## 2. 24 October 2017 HR email — independent contemporaneous functional context

CLIN-001793  **Source:** Gmail, sent by Liam Moloney to Pauline McDonagh and Deborah Walsh, subject `Re: Medical Assessment`, 24 October 2017.

CLIN-001794  Liam told HR that he had had to stop the medication about three or four weeks earlier because it was affecting his eyesight; the Parkinson's nurse took him off it for about 12 days and reintroduced it at a lower dosage. He reported that the lower dose did not cover all symptoms and that the nurse had just advised increasing the dose again while monitoring vision.

CLIN-001795  The email records:

CLIN-001796  > `apart from the vision, I am physically very much improved, you wouldn't believe there was ever much wrong with me.`

CLIN-001797  and:

CLIN-001798  > `The joy of being able to move about normally and function unchallenged has been the equivalent of living in a miracle.`

CLIN-001799  At the same time Liam recorded that he had a Podiatry appointment on Thursday, Physiotherapy on Friday and the Parkinson's nurse on Monday.

CLIN-001800  ### Source-status

CLIN-001801  This email corroborates, to a workplace recipient and for a practical occupational purpose, that the neurological medication regime had produced a profound physical improvement by October 2017, while visual toxicity was forcing withdrawal/reduction/re-escalation decisions.

CLIN-001802  It is particularly useful because it was not written for litigation, complaint or retrospective reconstruction.

---

CLIN-001803  ## 3. Present patient recollection — phenomenological clarification

CLIN-001804  Liam now clarifies that the improvement during procyclidine titration felt like a profound release of longstanding tension that had become almost a constant background `sound` in his bodily experience. He recalls being able to `walk with power` and experiencing the improvement as almost perfect apart from residual issues such as hand cramps and visual disturbance.

CLIN-001805  He further recalls that when procyclidine was withdrawn, the **first pain to return** was the focal pain on the outside of the left foot, the same pain he dates back to 2004, and that this focal pain persisted until March 2025.

CLIN-001806  ### Source-status

CLIN-001807  The present recollection adds phenomenological detail not fully expressed in the surviving October emails. In particular, the claim that the outside-left-foot pain was the *first* symptom to return should remain tagged as present patient recollection unless and until a contemporaneous clinical note, email or witness source independently confirms that precise sequence.

CLIN-001808  The contemporaneous October emails do strongly support the broader state: dramatic improvement on Kemadrin/procyclidine, planned withdrawal to test effect, deterioration during withdrawal, foot restlessness during withdrawal, and re-escalation because the lower dose failed to control all symptoms.

---

CLIN-001809  ## 4. Clinical significance to be tested, not assumed

CLIN-001810  The clean documentary point is not that procyclidine proved the feet were dystonia and not that procyclidine and levodopa are interchangeable.

CLIN-001811  It is narrower:

CLIN-001812  > **Neurology's own treatment regime materially altered the patient's physical state, and the contemporaneous withdrawal experiment produced deterioration that included the feet.**

CLIN-001813  Once that information existed, the neurological significance of treatment effects in the feet became a question requiring clinical interpretation.

CLIN-001814  The later record should therefore be examined for whether Neurology documented what the medication-foot interaction meant, whether it changed the earlier Podiatry/Neurology allocation, and whether it entered the criteria governing prolonged dopaminergic deferral.

# DOCUMENT 08 — Medical Assessment Email — 2017-10-24

CLIN-001815  # 24 October 2017 — Re: Medical Assessment

CLIN-001816  **Source type:** primary contemporaneous patient email  
CLIN-001817  **Author:** Liam Moloney  
CLIN-001818  **Recipients:** employer HR recipients (names retained in original Gmail; redacted here as non-essential)  
CLIN-001819  **Date/time:** 24 October 2017  
CLIN-001820  **Gmail message ID:** `15f4eacd53c5073c`  
CLIN-001821  **Provenance:** read directly from the connected Gmail account. This page is a redacted transcription of the patient-authored message body. No analytical findings are made on this page.

---

CLIN-001822  Hi Pauline,

CLIN-001823  How are you? That date and time is fine by me to see the doctor.

CLIN-001824  By way of query I wonder would you be able to outline the process from here on?

CLIN-001825  I suppose I should give you a brief update as to where things are for me at the moment.

CLIN-001826  You may already know that I had to stop taking my medication about three or four weeks ago.

CLIN-001827  The particular meds that I was on were eating away at my eyesight, a bit startling it was.

CLIN-001828  So the Parkinson's nurse took me off it for about 12 days and then reintroduced at a lower dosage.

CLIN-001829  Initially the eyesight returned to normal. Then when I started back on the medication even the lower dose was playing with my vision.
CLIN-001830  So I started wearing glasses. That is about two weeks ago.

CLIN-001831  Unfortunately the lower dose doesn't tick off all of the symptoms so I need the higher dose, or some compromise of sorts. So the Nurse just rang me now and has said to rise the dose, keep wearing the glasses and get back to him on Monday coming and he will assess things then.

CLIN-001832  So that's this moment in time.
CLIN-001833  I have a podiatry appointment on Thurs morning, Physio Friday morning and the nurse on Monday then...kept moving anyway!!

CLIN-001834  On the upshot, apart from the vision, I am physically very much improved, you wouldn't believe there was ever much wrong with me. The joy of being able to move about normally and function unchallenged has been the equivalent of living in a miracle.

CLIN-001835  One concern I have is whether or not I will have to wear glasses all the time because this will mean I need my glasses fitted with protection in the cleanroom. I think the company do this but you may know more...
CLIN-001836  And that's about it for the moment.

CLIN-001837  I'll be there at 2.44 on the 3rd November to see the Doc.
CLIN-001838  Hope to here from you meanwhile,

CLIN-001839  Kind regards,
CLIN-001840  Liam

---

CLIN-001841  **Redaction / scope note:** The original Gmail message includes the quoted prior HR scheduling email and corporate confidentiality footer. Those are not reproduced here because they contain third-party contact details and add no patient-authored medical content. The original message remains the controlling source.

CLIN-001887  April 2018-04-24th
CLIN-001888  [Patient] finds concentration is good. He can play at a really, really high level “Countdown” on an ongoing basis. He continues doing this online - as the best over-30 player online, in the world
CLIN-001889  But he’ll very “easily loose train of thought and when the concentration is gone - - it’s gone”.
CLIN-001890  [Patient]’s main concerns are physical:
CLIN-001891  He is having foot cramps and hand cramps. It is nearly always the LEFT hand.
CLIN-001892  Two weeks ago , while walking up the stairs, he pulled a muscle in the RIGHT chest.
CLIN-001893  He has had a crick in the neck and shoulder e.g. on stretching to look for a car parking space.
CLIN-001894  The pain and discomfort is worse than he’d expect.
CLIN-001895  He knows that this should only last a day or two – but it can get to him for c 2-3 weeks. It is a “ball of pain” that moves.
CLIN-001896  [Patient] is very cold sensitive.
CLIN-001897  Hand swing is good.
CLIN-001898  He feels that the gait is a bit off – he lacks both “fluency and speed”, for someone who he feels was good beforehand.
CLIN-001899  The relief from tension is fantastic “It’s nice to be able to walk 20metres” as he says “without tension”.
CLIN-001900  Transitions on floor surfaces are now OK e.g. going from carpet to tiles.
CLIN-001901  Sleep is good.
CLIN-001902  Movement in bed is as good as ever it was.
CLIN-001903  Anxiety has subsided.
CLIN-001904  Toe supports on both feet are a help .
CLIN-001905  Nails are pared back.
CLIN-001906  Bowels are “right as rain”. Movements are fairly uniform and they are formed - - he is NOT having to push.
CLIN-001907  Two-and-a-half weeks ago he went to his general practitioner with a chest infection or at least a cough of 2 different semiologies. He was given reflux medications at that stage.
CLIN-001908  [Movement Disorder Specialist] had explained why he would probably find it easier to stop smoking than other people. The smoking is 11-12 per day and he knows we would be very keen on him trying to stop.
CLIN-001909  Vision is “excellent”, except – it’s more work than it used to be - - it’s like he has focus and concentrate.
CLIN-001910  He put on weight - - - but he is not bothered about this. I was a bit bothered though initially, but it is very stable and he’s happier at this level of weight currently.
CLIN-001911  Rivotril - - he has pains in upper back and pains in the neck - - - He doesn't know the dose but by the description I think it is 0.5 mg.
CLIN-001912  He would get 14 tablets a month and [Patient] will take a half tablet - - - he’s had 35 in the last 9 months – he will take a half of the 0.5mg. He’ll sometimes use it for sleep. It really helps the neck and back by the morning – and he’ll be better the next day for having taken it the night before.
CLIN-001913  ===============
CLIN-001914  Our Nurse [Parkinson's Nurse] had suggested stopping Procyclidne for two weeks in Sept /Oct 2017 (regarding the blurring of the vision – [Patient] had seen me that day), but when he did that - - the first thing he noticed changing and changing rapidly was the return on the foot, of the outside left corn.
CLIN-001915  Symptoms got worse a bit.
CLIN-001916  (He remains on the Azilect)
CLIN-001917  But he was more “shifty” “unrelaxed” off the Proclyidine,
CLIN-001918  He had been off of it for a full two weeks.
CLIN-001919  He returned to Ailect at 2.5mg TDS-QDS
CLIN-001920  ---------------------------
CLIN-001921  I’m delighted to note he is back at work in Shipping with Integer Medical. He feels he’s doing well with the physical task but also in a partial Health and Safety role too, and the cognitive aspects of it.
CLIN-001922  He summarises it in one important statement – “I enjoy being alive.”
CLIN-001923  He feels he does have a bit of irritability – (but he seems fine to me.)
CLIN-001924  [Patient] has some probably piriformis pain on the left side. It’s about 1x per week for a couple of hours.
CLIN-001925  He does vary the Procylidine a bit, this is unintentionally – and if he does miss it he will feel tighter in the left bottom/? Piriformis later.
CLIN-001926  .================
CLIN-001927  Current Rx:
CLIN-001928  1 x 1mg Azilect
CLIN-001929  4 x ½ half tablets of Procylidine i.e ½ of the 5mg = 2.5mg QDS (he is varying this a bit)
CLIN-001930  Co Q10 - - - he takes about 200mg per day.
CLIN-001931  Folate 5mg
CLIN-001932  Vit D.
CLIN-001933  Vit B
CLIN-001934  Rivotril - - he has pains in upper back and pains in the neck - - - He doesn't know the dose but by the description I think it is 0.5 mg.
CLIN-001935  He would get 14 tablets a month and [Patient] will take a half tablet - - - he’s had 35 in the last 9 months – he will take a half of the 0.5mg. He’ll sometimes use it for sleep. It really helps the neck in /back by the morning.
CLIN-001936  He is off the PPi currently – it didn’t anything much for the recent cough.
CLIN-001937  ===============
CLIN-001938  Exam note:
CLIN-001939  [Patient] actually tends to move a little while he is sitting in the chair. It is not dyskinetic as such-and it is more than would be customarily expected in the hypokinetic disorder that it iPD. Obviously it is probably much better than him being frozen and bradykinetic. I certainly don't think this is an " overtreatment" phenomenology" in that it reflects the improvement in tension that he describes, and [Patient] describes that he was "always fidgety".
CLIN-001940  We did a video of him today both of the hand movements and also the gait.
CLIN-001941  There is bradykinesia however it is not bad. The walk is actually very good. He has a good arm swing without having to concentrate on it at all. Certainly the left-hand occasionally does "collide" with the leg but it is really relatively excellent.
CLIN-001942  ================
CLIN-001943  Impression
CLIN-001944  Obviously he could be very much better if we would actually give him the levodopa or another dopamine agonist ---however for a very clearly set out reasons, we do not want to do that – at least for quite some time.
CLIN-001945  He probably isn't going to be very tolerant of being given more and more procyclidine given the visual issues previously with it.
CLIN-001946  He is still keen that we might at some stage increase the Procyclidine and we will try sooner or later - - probably increasing one timing at a time.
CLIN-001947  Incidentally, while our overarching interest with procyclidine is to avoid visual issues or cogntive difficulties, he was asking me as regards whether there will be cognitive consequences from being on the procyclidine? In point of fact if there are cognitive effects from procyclidine (and younger people tolerate it generally very well), they are pure "acute or transient effects" i.e. it is not doing any cognitive damage to him whatsoever.
CLIN-001948  As regards the Rivotril, in other circumstances we would be deeply concerned about this. However it is relatively infrequent and he is not taking an excessive amount of it. It is relatively long acting and therefore it has are much less addictive potential. It controls very well aspect of his Parkinsons  There is another particular justification for this:
CLIN-001949  It relates to the pains and cramps and spasms and that "ball of pain" in the neck that can go down the back.
CLIN-001950  [Patient] will have the ordinary aches and pains and “cricks” that all 40-year-olds have. However in the last 10 years it has begun to be significantly understood that people with Parkinson’s have a much more "magnified" experience of pain than everybody else.
CLIN-001951  Therefore what he describes is not unreasonable or unusual - although it sounds different to lots of other 40-year-olds, who might have some degree of degenerative disc changes: Because the Rivotril relaxes this to a certain degree, I think it is reasonable to let him have it in much the way he is taking.
CLIN-001952  In simple terms we are much more willing to tolerate in him then we will are in other people. The simple reason for this is because cognitively even though he describes how he can lose his train of thought, he is functioning at a particularly very high level.
CLIN-001953  I was initially a bit concerned about the weight but he is not worried. I would just ask them to keep a weekly record of the weight because I do not want it heavier than he is at the moment.
CLIN-001954  Recommendations
CLIN-001955  As detailed above  (I forgot to discuss running the Genetics with [Patient] today I do apologise --- – but he’ll have a copy of this at home and I’ll explain everything here:
CLIN-001956  Obviously I’d be particularly interested to know – but this is certainly not about satisfying my wanting to know: I sort of want to delay it - - but basically if at any time he wants [Patient] done – for any reason - we will run it. The logic of delaying is as follows:  It doesn’t particularly change current management – so there is no great loss in “not knowing” currently right now. I’d probably prefer run a Whole Genome Sequence in time to come, as opposed to the 30 gene panel or the Specific PARK 2 PRKN which we/Prof Healy suspect is the cause – which is what we might do now. I think if we leave it longer - - - and if we were having to be doing WGS (assuming negative PARK2 /Panel) that there will be a more evolved library of associated genes known of by then, rather than if we do it now.  But there is a reasonable logic to doing the isolated test for PARK2 PRKN/or the 30 gene panel now (and indeed Prof Healy would be happy with that approach) , if [Patient] would like? If he decides he wants it done at any time, if [Patient] might send me or [Parkinson's Nurse] a note – we’ll get it arranged.

CLIN-001963  Nov 2019-11-14th
CLIN-001964  (It has become the understandable preference of some of the General Practitioners to have shorter form letters and therefore all previous details are as per older letters as previously supplied)
CLIN-001965  Dear [GP],
CLIN-001966  I was delighted to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.
CLIN-001967  In Sept 2019:
CLIN-001968  We had seen [Patient] at the request of the medical assessment unit team on 17 September.
CLIN-001969  He described a hot patch on the left head and the parietal region 4-5 months and [Patient] had seen [GP] and was diagnosed with C2 problems (very likely correct – i.e. Gr Occipital neuralgia)).
CLIN-001970  However the real reason he was there in September was an acute issue with the right shoulder with right shoulder pain. By 17 September this had been ongoing for 10 days. He was not sure how it came on. However it was swiftly worsening. The pain started mild-but then it got bad. Nothing would relieve it. Lying down though was 100% effective and indeed it was still helping him by 10 days after onset. CLIN-002190  In essence, most Parkinson's disease disorders are disorders of a protein called Synuclein or how Synuclein is handled - - whereas the Parkinson's plus disorders can be disorders of protein Synculein, or range of other protein disorders e.g. very prominently protein Tau.
CLIN-002191  It is an unpleasant analogy - and of course this is a very sensitive and deeply personal subject--- however an effective practical analogy which I have heard patients come up with themselves is perhaps the nature of: "rust" and rust “spreading”.

CLIN-002206  ---I use this example to explain where a person may have a range of different genes, many of them are currently unknown but which confer “susceptibilities” to Parkinson's. Depending on the combination of susceptibilities a person has genetically, then these, and various components of environment can contribute to the development of a Parkinson's disorder. This is a theory that probably applies to most people who actually develop Parkinson's disease “sporadically” or “idiopathically” who may have susceptibility genes that we do not understand the role of as yet or known genes with variations (e.g. in the LRRK2/ SNCA / GBA genes), as well as a range of possible environmental factors. #3---what I term for [Patient]’s understanding as "weak genetics"
CLIN-002207  ---by this we mean the classical autosomal recessively inherited disorders that can occur within a family but which would be very unlikely to affect other family members. It is quite plausible that [Patient] may have one of these disorders as the cause for his Parkinsons/Parkinsonisms.

CLIN-002211  Genetic Testing in Parkinson's Disease
CLIN-002212  Genetic testing has recently become available for the parkin and PINK1 genes. Parkin is a large gene and testing is difficult. At the current stage of understanding, testing is likely to give a meaningful result only for people who develop the condition before the age of 30 years. PINK1 appears to be a rare cause of inherited Parkinson's disease. A small percentage (~2 percent) of those developing the condition at an early age appear to carry mutations in the PINK1 gene. Genetic testing for the PARK7, SNCA and LRRK2 genes is also available.
CLIN-002213  Individuals and families who are interested in having genetic testing can learn more about their risk for Parkinson's disease and the availability and accuracy of genetic testing for this disease by setting up an appointment with a genetics health professional. Genetic professionals work as members of health care teams providing information and support to individuals or families who have genetic disorders or may be at risk for inherited conditions. Genetic professionals can discuss the risks, benefits and limitations of available genetic testing for Parkinson's disease. See: Frequently Asked Questions about Genetic Counseling

CLIN-002325  Neurology Notes 2021 - Redacted Source Page
CLIN-002326  Diagnostic Governance in Monogenic Parkinsonism
CLIN-002327  Year-split redacted longitudinal neurology material
CLIN-002328  Redaction and preparation note. This page contains redacted source material assigned to 2021. It is split by year for publication. Where the source extract did not print an exact clinic date, year assignment is based on internal chronological context and neighbouring dated entries.
CLIN-002329  Source extract 1
CLIN-002330  Neurology Clinic – Oct 2021-10-27th
CLIN-002331  Dear [GP],
CLIN-002332  It was a pleasure to see [Patient] back at neurology outpatient’s clinic today.
CLIN-002333  Of note, [Patient] has started doing a mediation course - which is going quite well.
CLIN-002334  As regards current status, [Patient] would describe that yesterday was a particularly very good day.
CLIN-002335  Going back to September 2021, [Patient] had felt a bit undertreated. He had contacted [Parkinson's Nurse] Borwne and [Patient] was given what would be viewed in fairness as a very tiny dose of Sinemet. That was 62.5 mg twice daily (12.5/50mg BD) as the first start of a Levodopa drug.
CLIN-002336  In the context of doing the course, it is a bother to him going between the reading glasses and the distance glasses. Therefore he had made an appointment for varifocals with the optician. He had slightly high pressures apparently at the optician. [Patient] then contacted [Parkinson's Nurse] about that and [Parkinson's Nurse] suggested that we would remove the Kemadrin and replace it with the Sinemet (for the ocular pressure).
CLIN-002337  [Patient] thinks that this might allow the eyes be somewhat better. [Patient] just had another intraocular pressure test yesterday with the optician and the pressure was still a bit raised (which was 23 and 24 where 22 would be the optimal maximum, they explained and 2 years previously the level had been 18).
CLIN-002338  [Patient] is obviously hoping that the Sinemet change with the increased dose now to 4 times per day will more than compensate for the Kemadrin removal and he is tolerating the Sinemet introduction and escalate well..
CLIN-002339  The weight is fine he explains. He is not specifically dieting.
CLIN-002340  To me, he looks like he has lost a fair amount of weight. He is still carrying some central adiposity, however it is not bad compared with before.
CLIN-002341  In terms of temperament, [Patient] would feel relaxed and peaceful in himself.
CLIN-002342  Obviously one of the biggest questions with the Sinemet was what are the impulse control disorder (ICD) elements like? (In fairness most of the impulse control problems that we see tends to be more with the synthetic agents like the neupro, and the pramipexole and the ropinirole). He describes a slight intensity may be in the first 3 or 4 days of the Sinemet starting and he had noticed a bit of change.
CLIN-002343  (ICD elements can be alcohol, increased spending, surreptitious spending, feeling of lack of control, wanting to buy things, obsessional thoughts).
CLIN-002344  Nevertheless that does seem to have settled but he (unlike many people with impulse control disorders) still retains very good insight into it and I think we can depend on him. That may not always be the case and [Patient] knows this. The alcohol is still gone.
CLIN-002345  On the other side of the impulse control issues, a part of that can be that tobacco use and cannabis use were recorded; quantities are redacted as non-essential for publication. (most smokers who get Parkinsons seem to stop – likely for lack of dopminerigc reward reinforcement of tobacco)
CLIN-002346  Impression
CLIN-002347  At the current time [Patient] seems to be in a relatively good place. In many respects in life I am always telling people that we “have to live in the day”. That is obviously what is important to QoL. Nevertheless we also do have to have a responsibility to the future. He is well aware of the impulse control disorder concerns that we have with the Sinemet but the impulse control element of things seems to him to be “well under wraps”.
CLIN-002348  There is also the Chorea.
CLIN-002349  The other thing that I am a little bit concerned about is that effectively the Sinemet dose has become 62.5 mg x4 times per day rather quickly. Compared with other people with Parkinson’s who would be self increasing the Sinemet rather quickly, the circumstances here were somewhat justified:
CLIN-002350  The Kemadrin was being removed as it seemed that it needed to go.
CLIN-002351  But the thing that has to be understood here is that his effective dose of Sinemet is probably higher than other people on Sinemet 62.5 mg x4 times per day because he is actually on the Comtess already. (Conversely most Sinemet is after food)
CLIN-002352  Therefore what I am trying to articulate here is that I do not want the Sinemet rising significantly above where we are. Already he has had the slightest awareness of chorea while playing darts.
CLIN-002353  It is a general principle of Parkinson’s management all over the world that we try and spare Sinemet for as long as possible in people who are very young (and [Patient] is still just 44). Additionally when Sinemet is used, the aim should be to keep the dosing as absolutely minimal as possible.
CLIN-002354  One issue that I have to acknowledge that [Patient] is hopeful and looking forward to is that the “cognitive effects” that can happen with Kemadrin (which I have never been really worried too much about especially in [Patient]) but which he has always been aware of may improve with the removal of the Kemadrin.
CLIN-002355  I would guess that the pressure in the eyes will probably subside over the next few weeks and he can have the
CLIN-002356  eye pressure checked again. If the eye pressure is still raised then obviously it is a question of a glaucomatous situation and likely then quite independent of the Kemadrin.
CLIN-002357  He has already paid for the varifocal.
CLIN-002358  He might as well get them because one way or another, given his normal age anyway, it is expected that he would naturally be requiring distance vision glasses along with reading glasses. Nevertheless it will be interesting to see does he actually notice anything different subjectively in vision, with the apparent reduction in pressure?
CLIN-002359  [Patient] has been fully counselled on all of the above and indeed presently will have a copy of this directly at home.
CLIN-002360  He mentioned about DBS today. As I have reminded him, the DBS is only ever for the tremor element. It will not help with anything else. He will likely always remain a candidate for DBS and we can look into this ad any time if he wishes to proceed with that
CLIN-002361  Follow-up:
CLIN-002362  12 months
CLIN-002363  Will review with ANP [Parkinson's Nurse] in interim if issues.
CLIN-002364  I do hope this is of assistance.
CLIN-002365  If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.
CLIN-002366  Thank you,
CLIN-002367  Yours sincerely,
CLIN-002368  [Consultant Neurologist]

CLIN-002370  Neurology Notes 2022 - Redacted Source Page
CLIN-002371  Diagnostic Governance in Monogenic Parkinsonism
CLIN-002372  Year-split redacted longitudinal neurology material
CLIN-002373  Redaction and preparation note. This page contains redacted source material assigned to 2022. It is split by year for publication. Where the source extract did not print an exact clinic date, year assignment is based on internal chronological context and neighbouring dated entries.
CLIN-002374  Source extract 1
CLIN-002375  Neurology clinic
CLIN-002376  Dear [GP],
CLIN-002377  It was a pleasure to see [Patient] back at clinic today.
CLIN-002378  [Patient] explains he is getting on very well.
CLIN-002379  He’d bought a dog this time last year. He feels his physical activity is now much better. He is walking 2.5-3 hrs per day.
CLIN-002380  He finished his qualification as a mediator and is due to qualify.
CLIN-002381  He is starting another course presently and he will be doing a healing practitioner course.
CLIN-002382  Most mornings the feet take 20-25 mins to warm up. They are a bit sore - with the corns and calluses feeling like a “coronal pain” around the edges. He wonders is it neuralgic?
CLIN-002383  He has cramping but it is almost “zero percent”. There have been maybe just 3-4 of small finger locking crampings in the last few months.
CLIN-002384  The Meds are above.
CLIN-002385  Small Sinemets are optional. He takes some Diazepam in lieu of the missing Rivotril, but that will be back soon. He was not taking rivotril every night anyway
CLIN-002386  He has to adjust Sinemet according to expected demands e.g. for the playing darts.
CLIN-002387  In terms of other issues
CLIN-002388  He has lost a load of weight. This is with the dog and with intention.
CLIN-002389  Sleep is generally OK.
CLIN-002390  Bowels are regular and he is going every day.
CLIN-002391  Alcohol intake is about 4 pints every 2nd day and he feels this is much more under control. If he went over 4 pints he would not be able to manage it as [Patient] explained.
CLIN-002392  Impression.
CLIN-002393  Things are going well here.
CLIN-002394  Looking at [Patient] today, it is at most "subtle" in terms of excess movement. I can see it because "I am looking for it". It is not intrusive and it is not excessive. However there are very subtle elements of slightly excessive movement. In no way would it be fair to characterise this as ordinary wiggling dyskinesia but there is just that slight little excessive movement. Nevertheless, it is at a very acceptable level and it certainly is not intrusive; embarrassing nor bothersome for him.
CLIN-002395  However it has to be remembered that this is happening with a very minimal dose of levodopa built into that Stalevo and he had more difficulty with Stalevo 75 whereby he well remembers being very obviously dyskinetic (the chorea).
CLIN-002396  Recommendations.
CLIN-002397  It will only be a short period of time before Rivotril comes back available. These are supply chain issues that are plaguing medicine at the moment. He actually has lots and lots of Rivotril at home. His Rivotril usage was may be half the days per month at a maximum. In a similar way his usage of the diazepam (that he has been given to supplement the loss of the Rivotril has been pretty minimal). For technical reasons, if he wants to take a benzodiazepine at night to assist with sleep; I would really prefer it would be the Rivotril. There are a range of reasons for this. The diazepam is very short acting. I would just prefer that it would be the Rivotril that would be used as it is safer. This is only if he needs it.
CLIN-002398  We have had a long chat about how things are likely to change over time with dyskinesia. Most people will prefer the “experience” of dyskinesia as troublesome as it is , as opposed to being the frozen or akinetic unable to move.
CLIN-002399  We had a long chat about the logic in previous considerations of Sinemet usages. 50% of all people on Sinemet will have dyskinesia within 5 years. But delayed start studies and other work proves that while the Sinemet fuels or power the dyskinesia that it does not “cause” it. The cause is the impaired ability of the brain in handling normal dopamine.
CLIN-002400  While this may give more trouble in time to come with increased dopamine that may become needed - - as above most individuals will “prefer” the excessive movement over being akinetically rigid.
CLIN-002401  He had done chemistry at college and [Patient] asked about the way different chemical compounds and indeed pesticides can maybe be associated with inducing Parkinson's disease? He would have taken a smell of certain compounds at various different times. The short answer here is that it is really, really difficult to see or imagine how he could have chemically induced his own Parkinson's.
CLIN-002402  When we see it, really the only place we see it happening in usual society is very rarely certain "drug dealers" involved in the compounding of certain agents, developing this sort of Parkinsonian scenario. It is unlikely that
CLIN-002403  his is a toxic Parkinsonism. Obviously his own situation is very different to the usual drug induced Parkinson's that we also see from medicinal compounds such as in the management of schizophrenias.
CLIN-002404  [Patient] asked how far away from a cure. He will forgive me please for being "realistic". Unfortunately things are still a very long way away. Our biggest hopes and expectations had centred majorly on antibody therapy-antusynculein nuclear antibody therapy. 2 major trials were published in the summer and unfortunately these did not go well at all (Editorial from NEJM attached). Basically the treatment failed in terms of clearing the pathological substrate of Parkinson's disease – the synuclein. Nevertheless, while it is not meant to be a consolation, the very fact of the magnitude of the population that are affected by Parkinson's disease will always ensure that there will always be massive research into this on an ongoing basis.
CLIN-002405  I have not made any changes today.
CLIN-002406  Follow-up:
CLIN-002407  In 1 year Neurology Clinic.
CLIN-002408  I do hope this is of assistance.
CLIN-002409  If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.
CLIN-002410  Thank you,
CLIN-002411  Yours sincerely,
CLIN-002412  [Consultant Neurologist]

CLIN-002419  Neurology clinic
CLIN-002420  Dear [GP],

CLIN-002422  Notwithstanding all of the very significant concerns explored below over about an hour and 20 mins, I thought he was looking well. CLIN-002423  [Patient] explains the medications as outlined above. Vit D and Folate are gone. There are no other major changes.
CLIN-002424  [Patient] explains the last 6 months have been a journey. He underwent a process with a Spiritual Healer, A remote non-medical healing session was described; identifying and non-essential details are redacted. She told him that his Parkinsons was a construct was set up to torture himself
CLIN-002425  He ground to an absolute halt with foot pain in both feet immediately after the Healing. There has been three bad bouts where he gave up in the last 6 months. But he has tried his best to prevail
CLIN-002426  The big difficulty he had was pain with walking. But he will say that the pain is now gone. It is understandably difficult to describe but as: [Patient] states: it more that the attention to walking that is is “painful” to him in the evenings and difficulty walking. It is the attention to walking that he needs to put to it that he describes as painful.
CLIN-002427  While this is of both feet, repeatedly over the consult he described symptoms including cramping in the LLL (which leads to the question of a Parkinsonian dystonia whether an ON or OFF dystonia (clearly more likely an OFF dystonia).
CLIN-002428  Before the Energetic Healing he was walking the dog 3-4 x per day. He was fine and enthusiastic. Since the energetic healing he has had to scale it back.
CLIN-002429  He has/had/has a coronoa of pain around the edge of the feet, which he explains is a little bit of a bother – and I think there are elements of this still present. The second walk of the day is fine and he is energised. Dinner is at 5. He feels the walk is between 70-90% of normal in the evening walking. He can’t do a fourth walk at night at all at all and could not even consider it.
CLIN-002430  He explained that the Woman who had healed his Parkinson's, said she could not heal his foot pain.
CLIN-002431  The foot pain was the major problem.
CLIN-002432  [Patient] feels that his major problem is “Psychologising about his walk”.
CLIN-002433  He checked on Chat GPT and he found that mice exposed to foot pain get Parkinsons more often.
CLIN-002434  He feels he needs to unlearn behaviours.
CLIN-002435  [Patient] is in bare feet in the morning and he feels the shoes hurt the feet (implying that there is still an element of pain?).
CLIN-002436  But he can walk after that.
CLIN-002437  He feels the pain is of “heavy psychological burden”, and that the walk “psychologically consumes him”.
CLIN-002438  The third walk is a psychological mountain as [Patient] explained, and he can’t do the fourth walk at night, and he believes his inability to do this is psychological.
CLIN-002439  [while psychological aspects will always worsen Parkinsons e.g. in the simplest setting stress making a tremor or an embarrassing OFF situation worse; or in other settings the embarrassment of Dyskinesia making Dyskinesia worse - - -I don’t think this is some simply psychological process or Functional Neurological Disorder.]
CLIN-002440  He feels his rigidity and tightness is gone and now he has a more flowing “chorea movement” in the morning (but that Choreaform movement cannot be simply characterised as a “good thing” just because it is associated with freedom of movement).
CLIN-002441  [Patient] explains that feels the mood is good. He starts each and every day, not based on how things were the day before and he finds it astonishing that he can get on with life in this manner despite everything. He explains the outlook is positive, and the mood is positive - even “great” as [Patient] describes.
CLIN-002442  He would say and clarify again that “the pain is not pain” but it is the “pain of the attention it takes to walk” with the major discomfort on the outside of the left foot.
CLIN-002443  It is usually OK in the day, unless he has been walking or after sexual activity during the day.
CLIN-002444  In terms of Diet: Dinner is the main and only definite meal of the day. There is no breakfast but if he rarely has medication he feels over medicated, i.e. that chorea is worse, and he’ll get pains in back and shoulder.
CLIN-002445  Examination of the gait. Superficially, the gait actually looks okay though I did see some stumbles. However when the gait is watched for longer, the arm swing is actually somewhat excessive. This could be characterised as mildly choreiform as he describes himself – i.e. a Choreaform dyskinesia. However again compared with the majority of people with Parkinson's who have a history of iPD for so long, and especially so compared with most younger people on Levodopa, this is actually relatively very minimal. Conversely you could see how a little extra could easily become too much.
CLIN-002446  Our Questions:
CLIN-002447  In terms of what the Lyrica does [Patient] feels it helps/helped with the foot pain. He had severe problems with the foot pain in the early years but then it subsided - really went away. But after the healing the foot pain became a major problem. He feels the foot pain is largely gone with the Lyrica currently.
CLIN-002448  Therefore to answer the question of why he can’t do the fourth walk? – [Patient] would answer that clawing is one part of it. He feels “its psychological, its social, its physical.” But basically he can’t do the fourth walk of the evening.
CLIN-002449  Alcohol and Cannabis and tobacco.
CLIN-002450  He has gone back to drinking daily about 4-5 pints in the evening each day since Jan / Feb 2023
CLIN-002451  [Patient] explains that of 14 rollies per day, 6 have cannabis.
CLIN-002452  Darts (only played 4 times in the last 6th months)
CLIN-002453  He wished to show a video but the first one he had was slow-mo video which actually looked OK. He didn’t feel this was a good illustration of what he meant.
CLIN-002454  He actually found the video that he meant: This showed him playing darts at 11 PM at night. There would have been alcohol on board and Levodopa would have been taken as Stalevo to facilitate this. His capacity at the darts while throwing was actually very very good. There were then some stumbles that looked like stumbles, but really could be nearly characterised as extra steps. Then as he comes back to standing still there was some mild excessive movement at the hips. However, compared with the vast majority of people with Parkinson's disease who have chorea, this would be considered exceptionally minimal at least in this video.
CLIN-002455  He feels this is not representative of most evening whereby he would be "crippled" with a “protective stance, lack of movement, and short steps”.
CLIN-002456  In essence, at least to me it sounded like he is describing typical OFF Parkinson's evening symptoms.
CLIN-002457  Basically though when he is going to play darts, he will have levodopa on board in the evening time having taken the Stalevo in the evening to facilitate the darts.
CLIN-002458  Impression
CLIN-002459  I’m nervous of certain aspects. The exercise quantum is great and really he looks fit and well.
CLIN-002460  But I’m concerns about the tobacco, but moreso about the cannabis and orders of magnitude more concerned about the alcohol at 4>5 drinks per night - - which is now on top of Lyrica at 600mg per day. It makes the interpretation of the whole situation really difficult.
CLIN-002461  I’m nervous of the diet, but it seems to be a hot meal per day with meat/veg and I’ll want him back on low dose folate 400mcirog (not the old 5mg), Vit D 800 and now I’d like thiamine 100microg. A Vit B complex with some B12 would probably be no harm too.
CLIN-002462  It is not in the nature of a “peripheral neuropathy” to come on literally overnight, including notwithstanding that he had had foot pains/issues many years before going back to the beginning.
CLIN-002463  [Patient] explains he wishes to entertain the view that the Parkinsons has been cured. CLIN-002464  From the bottom of my heart nothing would give me greater joy than to see a normalised DaTSCAN. While it would be embarrassing if there was a cause other than iPD, we have a larger interest in his diagnosis being right.
CLIN-002465  He cannot explain at all why he can’t walk in the evenings - but he it is not the pain in the evenings at least now. He feels it is the architecture of the walking. He feels the Lyrica has worked well for the pain/
CLIN-002466  He feels the smoothness of movement is fine for upto 10 hrs per day, but the evenings fails.
CLIN-002467  This is a mild excess of movement on evening he plays darts (but there will be the extra Levodopa on board those nights which were only 4 nights in the last 6 months), but it is a lack of movement on other evenings.
CLIN-002468  I think there could be a reasonable suspicion of that most of the problem of not being able to walk is the lack of Levodopa?
CLIN-002469  The point that I am trying to make to him is that he is already taking the 2nd Stalevo on the evenings that he is going to play darts. He feels that his walking is not perfect on these evenings, but the point is that as imperfect as it is, is the Stalevo is facilitating him playing the darts?
CLIN-002470  He then makes the point that "I am taking the Stalevo for "Pep in the Step" and "for pain".
CLIN-002471  I would feel that both of these experiential points can be easily understood in the context of how levodopa works in iPD:
CLIN-002472  In the first case, the fundamental purpose of the levodopa is quite literally to give people "pep in their step". More accurately it is meant to take them from being bradykenetic (slow in movement) to having an ability move. We characterise it as fluidity of movement and taken to excess, or minimal dosing in people sensitive to Sinemet that emerges as chorea. (People who don’t need Levodopa will never get chorea) It is the question of whether some Stalevo in the evening time regularly would facilitate that 4th walk (or Madopar or Sinemet of Sinemet CR).
CLIN-002473  It is a classic and well established feature of Parkinson's disease in the last 20 years that whatever pain syndrome a person may have (classically lumbar disc pain because it happens with the older age groups than [Patient]) that when a person is "OFF" from a Parkinson's perspective that all experiences of pain are worse. This is a well-established feature called a non-motor fluctuation of Parkinsons.
CLIN-002474  I would have felt that is probably why he feels he has an improvement in the pain symptoms in the evenings where he takes the Stalevo. This probably answers why he feels he is taking the Stalevo "for pain".
CLIN-002475  To acknowledge his own feelings in this regard, [Patient] explains that he does not believe that the DaTscan if it was repeated now would be the same. CLIN-002476  He would wish the DATSCSAN to be repeated to understand whether a cure has been achived.
CLIN-002477  [Patient] and I know each other for a very long time and I think it is fair to say that we get on very well. CLIN-002478  explainable by the Parkinson's, and him having very reasonable and justifiable concerns that a lot of this relates to the feet as a separate problem. The very first symptoms were in the feet all those years ago notwithstanding that the previous “peripheral neuropathy question”, seemed to subside.
CLIN-002479  Accordingly, in addition to this and to try to make sure that we are not missing very rare entities that have significant neuropathological overlaps with idiopathic Parkinson's disease (and indeed can be a cause of Parkinson's disease), we will be checking Alpha galactosidase levels which will be going to London. CLIN-002480  I should acknowledge that peripheral neuropathic symptoms are very very common in Parkinson's disease.
CLIN-002481  Therefore we will have Fabry checked for as well. I would ask that he’d be careful when reading about Fabry as the parallels with his own situation might make a person feel that this has to be the explanation. This is simply a possibility I’d like to look into.

CLIN-002484  I’d advise [Patient] to please reduce the alcohol at least by half if not more please.
CLIN-002485  The smoking doesn’t likely have a contribution to any of this but I’d like this reduced/removed more from a holistic general health perspective.
CLIN-002486  I can’t ask him to do more exercise, as what he is doing even if he can’t do the fourth walk is excellent.

# DOCUMENT 16 — Neurology Notes — 2024

CLIN-002690  Neurology Notes 2024 - Redacted Source Page
CLIN-002691  Diagnostic Governance in Monogenic Parkinsonism
CLIN-002692  Year-split redacted longitudinal neurology material

CLIN-002694  Source extract 1
CLIN-002695  Neurology note:
CLIN-002696  Dear [GP],
CLIN-002697  It was a pleasure to see [Patient] at clinic today.
CLIN-002698  The medication is as detailed above. This is largely similar. The Lyrica is 150 mg 4 times per day. Generally the Sinemet is on an empty stomach except for after dinner. He does not drink a lot of fluids specifically with the Sinemets, but he is drinking plenty during the day. As regards the liquid with Sinemet we tend to like a good volume of non-milky fluid being taken with this. Therefore a half a glass or a glass of the cordial that he likes, would be fine.
CLIN-002699  The Anxicalm dose is half a tablet - he is not sure how much taken at night. If and when the Rivotril becomes available, he would prefer and I think we would prefer as well that he would go back onto his old Rivotril please in lieu of the Anxicalm.
CLIN-002700  In general terms, [Patient] explains he is living within his boundaries. He’s generally well and he feels he’s functioning well within his day, but he does realise life is “a lot smaller”. E.g. it takes a long time to fold clothes - especially in the evenings - he’s very tired. Pumping tyres on the bike means that after 7 mins his toes will be curling in. He is down to darts 90 min per week down from 3 hrs per day. This is a function of the foot pain made worse by standing playing darts. The Lyrica removed the foot pain – the right foot is much better, there is still some in the left. But it isn’t relieving the toe curling in when playing darts. He is happy to walk or cycle miles. [Patient] doesn’t like wakening early in the morning (and he doesn’t like it if he wakens at night – a folded sheet will annoy him). He didn’t realise how much anxiety he had til the Lyrica. He doesn’t get moody (used get for 3-4 days as he used to have every 3-4 weeks) and he thinks it’s the Lyrica, [but also it could be from mental hygiene (and [Patient] is of course likely quite right in both respects).]. Most of the time he’s not dyskietic til after the first pint (of 4-5) at c 9PM. There was minimal dyskinesia of both hands walking but it looked close to normal.
CLIN-002701  [Patient] asked me about the Fabry testing. This had been sent last summer but unfortunately they do not send us the results directly unlike radiology and therefore I had not sent this out. I do apologise.
CLIN-002702  Basically Alpha galactosidase and the beta Hexosaminidase testing were all in the normal ranges for [Patient]. Therefore a Fabry disease is not the explanation for his Parkinson's.
CLIN-002703  Impression.
CLIN-002704  The question is whether we should change anything at the moment. Himself [Patient] would feel the medication is “spot on”.
CLIN-002705  There was no dyskinesia today.
CLIN-002706  Alcohol is 4-5 pints every night which he knows I feel is much too much.
CLIN-002707  I’d really prefer if he was on thiamine because of this.
CLIN-002708  Recommendations. I do feel that we should shift the evening c5PM Sinemet to before food with a good volume of water. (He’d doesn’t mind trying this but if its problematic he’ll go back to the way he was.) We have an option on a new COMPTESS like drug now.
CLIN-002709  The problem is that he may become excessively dyskinetic? The worry is that even on relatively minimal Sinemet (and it is minimal) we can see the mild dyskinesia in his hands when we look for it.
CLIN-002710  This is the classic “balancing problem” in terms of trying to help him in the evenings, versus, pushing him too far from a dopamine perspective and making him dyskinetic.
CLIN-002711  I’d prefer he’d been on the Thiamine please 100mg, but a family member assists with nutrition. But I don’t want him at risk of an alcohol related neuropathy. He knows I worry about the amount of alcohol.
CLIN-002712  When the Rivotril becomes available again, I’d be grateful if he could be switched back to this at night time please.
CLIN-002713  Follow-up:
CLIN-002714  In 1 year Neurology Clinic.
CLIN-002715  I do hope this is of assistance.
CLIN-002716  If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.
CLIN-002717  Thank you,
CLIN-002718  Yours sincerely,
CLIN-002719  [Consultant Neurologist]

# DOCUMENT 17 — Neurology Notes — 2025

CLIN-002759  Neurology Notes 2025 - Redacted Source Page
CLIN-002760  Diagnostic Governance in Monogenic Parkinsonism
CLIN-002761  Year-split redacted longitudinal neurology material
CLIN-002762  Redaction and preparation note. This page contains redacted source material assigned to 2025. It is split by year for publication. Where the source extract did not print an exact clinic date, year assignment is based on internal chronological context and neighbouring dated entries.
CLIN-002763  Source extract 1
CLIN-002764  * Neurology note:
CLIN-002765  Dear [GP],
CLIN-002766  It was a pleasure to see [Patient] at clinic today.
CLIN-002767  The medication is as detailed above. The major change is a reduction in the Lyrica from the maximum 600mg/day down to a total of 50mg 3 times per day. Thiamine is not really being taken anymore but that is less likely to matter because all alcohol finished on the 07/August/2024.
CLIN-002768  The one thing that is on [Patient]'s mind is foot pain.
CLIN-002769  I have gone through the other Parkinsons first.
CLIN-002770  The complete cessation of 4 or 5 pints of Guinness each day was associated with sudden and massive constipation. This is why he is doing the AllBran. It is absolutely necessary to him. If he didn't like the AllBran, we would suggest Movicol but he is happy with this and he would really prefer to avoid Movicol.
CLIN-002771  Wiggling dyskinesia is something he doesn’t notice hugely, but he sees a household pet appeared to notice the left foot movements. In actual fact, what he is describing in the afternoon in terms of the dyskinesia is a dyskinetic walk. That spread of the feet and feeling of movement of the feet and how he mimics, this is more consistent with dyskinesia. In the examination of his walk, the dyskinesia does become mildly more prominent. He tends to hold his hands together which is probably subconscious and traps them to a degree hiding the dyskinesia and appearing to normalise the appearance of the walk
CLIN-002772  People don’t comment on it though.
CLIN-002773  Sleeping is excellent. There is no dreaming remembered.
CLIN-002774  There isn’t much tremor.
CLIN-002775  He isn’t doing the darts anymore
CLIN-002776  As he observes, being seen in the morning time in clinic is not ideal because the morning time is quite simply the best time of the day for [Patient]. He does feel "more cornered" by the Parkinsons condition especially in the evening times.
CLIN-002777  The feet (but mainly the left):
CLIN-002778  Since 2005 he simple describes “my feet disturb me”. He hobbles out of bed. He takes the meds but the feet are too sore to tie the runners. Each morning it feels like “murder in the feet”, and there is discomfort. The first 150 footsteps are torture. He feels he’s festinating but from the pain. 500-600 steps are done in Wellies over rocks on the beach. To say “I won’t live with it” allows him put words on the situation. As regards the reduction in the Lyrica, [GP] had talked about
CLIN-002779  reducing this. [Patient] wasn’t sure why. But he reduced the Lyrica himself 6 months after [GP] left it where it was (probably from August September 2024 when he start the Healthy Life approach himself). Himself [Patient] had thought that the improvements that he had psychologically were from improvements in mental hygiene. As we acknowledged last year that most certainly could have been a factor in the anxiety element improvement. Because of those improvements, [Patient] had reduced the Lyrica.
CLIN-002780  Once the Parkinsons medications hit his system he does have a relief in the feelings in the feet.
CLIN-002781  Impression.
CLIN-002782  First of all, it is an absolutely excellent result that the alcohol is completely gone.
CLIN-002783  If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here, the alcohol being gone will hopefully slow the deterioration of that.
CLIN-002784  Just so we are clear, I have gone through my own letter of last year with [Patient]. I had not actually suggested or otherwise implied that the Lyrica be reduced. Most certainly it was noted that he was at the maximum dose of this in 2024, at 150mg 4 times per day equivalent to 600mg.
CLIN-002785  I suspect that he is psychologically much better as a function of all of the mental hygiene work he has done and continues to practice.
CLIN-002786  I do not think he really needs the Lyrica from the perspective of the psychology.
CLIN-002787  He knows very well that once the Sinemet hits his system that the feet feel better from a pain perspective. That is commonly described. Sinemet should not do anything for pain but it will relieve the experience of pain to at least some degree. For example if he had had old serious ankle or leg fractures that were still playing up Sinemet would probably make them feel better as well.
CLIN-002788  However, I do think he is missing the Lyrica from the perspective of the feet?
CLIN-002789  Quite simply, pain is not really a direct part of Parkinson's at all. Certainly if person has dystonia a person can have painful dystonia. However, the type of pain that he is describing is not a direct Parkinson's phenomenology.
CLIN-002790  But I have little doubt other than that if we increased the Sinemet, he would probably get some further relief from the pain. It would not likely go away. But there would be some relief. But the problem is that if we increase his Sinemet, then he will get much more severe dyskinesias.
CLIN-002791  Therefore, increasing "Parkinson's" medications will only be of limited help and probably associated with significant complications. I do not mind trying if [Patient] would like to try that.
CLIN-002792  But an avoidance of a dose increase of Parkinsons meds is why I would suggest a consideration somewhat increasing the Lyrica. His own feeling in this regard would be, that he feels that perhaps an element of this is how bad the Parkinson's is expected to be in the morning times - because he has not had medications for example overnight.
CLIN-002793  In this regard, there would be the option on giving him a slow release Sinemet at night time?
CLIN-002794  The last Sinemet in the evening - night time at the current time is taken at about 8 PM, and bed is at 1 AM and he wakens to get up at 0845 hrs.
CLIN-002795  As he explains he would be definitely open to trying this. I cannot promise [Patient] that this is going to work. Also it may be associated with intolerable dyskinesias.Furthermore it may promote night-time dreaming.
CLIN-002796  He knows all of this from before - but he is willing to try and I do think it is very reasonable to try.
CLIN-002797  The very first thing that he would be interested in trying - and it is entirely reasonable might be to try to wake up around about 08:00am and swallows the tablets while still in bed. I didn't suggest this myself because we do not like breaking people's sleep, but in reality it is what a lot of patients will do to "get themselves going". He actually doesn't mind trying this at all because he will have woken and gone to the toilet probably before 8 anyway.
CLIN-002798  First of all, we will start Sinemet controlled release for [Patient]. He is going to take this probably around somewhere between 12 midnight and 1AM - probably as close to going to bed as
CLIN-002799  is convenient - maybe around the time he takes his Anxicalm. The will be a half tablet of a “Half Sinemet CR”. This will be for about 2 months. Assuming he is tolerating that without difficulty then it can be moved to a whole tablet of a “Half Sinemet CR” taken at night time.
CLIN-002800  He was wondering about seeing a chronic pain specialist. It would be my own suspicion that they would probably recommend Amitriptyline or increasing the Lyrica. As he explains himself, it would be his view that at the 600mg of the Lyrica the pain was certainly not eliminated. Nevertheless, the concern is that he was doing relatively better on that then now.
CLIN-002801  Follow-up:
CLIN-002802  In 1 year Neurology Clinic.
CLIN-002803  I do hope this is of assistance.
CLIN-002804  If you have any questions or concerns please do not hesitate to contact me.
CLIN-002805  Thank you,
CLIN-002806  Yours sincerely,
CLIN-002807  [Consultant Neurologist]

CLIN-002871  **Subjective**

CLIN-002872  Patient attended the clinic for the first time having been diagnosed with parkinsons in April "my gait was very bad but it's not any more"

CLIN-002873  **Objective**

CLIN-002874  All nails long, o/x & onychomycotic. Bilateral tinea pedis interdigitally and on plantar metatarsal area. All nails slightly clubbed. Callus on L/5 MTPJ.

CLIN-002875  **Action**

CLIN-002876  Patient gave consent for treatment by student. Pre & Post op wipe. All nails cut, filled and reduced. Callus removed from L/5 MTPJ. Toe prop made from ottoform for bilateral 2-4. Patient advised to wear them if they're comfortable and remove if not. Patient has tight gastroc and soleus, given wall stretches (40seconds, 10reps, 3times a day).

CLIN-002877  **Plan**

CLIN-002878  Patient advised to use anti fungal powder for tinea pedis and anti fungal nail laquer for onychomycotic nails. Patient to return in 6months or call if any issues.

CLIN-002879  Treatment completed by Emma Scroope (4th Year Student)

Patient advised never to use chemical plasters on corn as they cause damage to the surrounding skin

CLIN-002894  **Plan**

CLIN-002895  Patient to return when insoles are ready for fitting and advised to call if any issues arise. Patient agreed not to use the acidic corn plasters again.
